    * that happens to have the same name\", which is the distinction that matters here.\n     */\n    private static long[] identity(Path p) throws CryptoException {\n        try {\n            Object dev = Files.getAttribute(p, \"unix:dev\", LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS);\n            Object ino = Files.getAttribute(p, \"unix:ino\", LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS);\n            if (!(dev instanceof Long d) || !(ino instanceof Long i)) {\n                throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                        \"filesystem does not report inode identity for \" + p);\n            }\n            return new long[] {d, i};\n        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException | IllegalArgumentException e) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                    \"inode identity could not be read for \" + p, e);\n        } catch (IOException e) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.KEY_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE,\n                    \"inode identity could not be read for \" + p, e);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Ownership re-check that reports rather than throws, for use after the read. */\n    private static boolean stillOwnedByMe(Path p) {\n        String me = System.getProperty(\"user.name\");\n        try {\n            return me != null && me.equals(Files.getOwner(p, LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS).getName());\n        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException | IOException e) {\n            return false;\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Verifies {@code file} is a private, non-symlink, owner-only regular file.\n     *\n     * @throws CryptoException naming the failed requirement in {@code getTechnicalDetail()}\n     */\n    public static void requirePrivateKeyFile(Path file) throws CryptoException {\n        PosixFileAttributes attrs = readPosix(file);\n        if (attrs.isSymbolicLink()) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                    \"key file is a symbolic link\");\n        }\n        if (!attrs.isRegularFile()) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                    \"key file is not a regular file\");\n        }\n        Set<PosixFilePermission> perms = attrs.permissions();\n        for (PosixFilePermission p : perms) {\n            switch (p) {\n                case OWNER_READ:\n                case OWNER_WRITE:\n                    break;\n                default:\n                    /* Group/other access, or any execute bit on a data file: refuse. */\n                    throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                            \"key file permissions grant more than owner read/write: \"\n                                    + java.nio.file.attribute.PosixFilePermissions.toString(perms));\n            }\n        }\n        int mode = unixMode(file);\n        if ((mode & (S_ISUID | S_ISGID)) != 0) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                    \"key file has a set-id bit set\");\n        }\n        requireOwns(file, \"key file\");\n        requireSecureDirectory(file.toAbsolutePath().getParent());\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Verifies a directory cannot be written to by anyone but its owner, allowing the sticky-bit\n     * convention for shared temporary directories.\n     *\n     * @throws CryptoException if the directory is writable by group or other without a sticky bit, is not\n     *     owned by this user, is not a directory, or is a symlink\n     */\n    public static void requireSecureDirectory(Path dir) throws CryptoException {\n        if (dir == null) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                    \"directory is missing\");\n        }\n        Path abs = dir.toAbsolutePath();\n        /* Walk from the shortest prefix down: a private file inside an open directory is not private. */\n        for (Path component = abs; component != null; ) {\n            PosixFileAttributes attrs = readPosix(component);\n            if (attrs.isSymbolicLink()) {\n                throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                        \"key file directory path traverses a symbolic link: \" + component);\n            }\n            boolean isRoot = component.getParent() == null;\n            if (!isRoot && !attrs.isDirectory()) {\n                throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                        \"key file directory path component is not a directory: \" + component);\n            }\n            int mode = unixMode(component) & PERMS_MASK;\n            boolean sticky = (mode & S_ISVTX) != 0;\n            /*\n             * Group and other write access are treated as one condition, because they are the same\n             * threat: somebody else can unlink or replace an entry. The sticky bit is what removes that\n             * power - only the owner of an entry may delete or rename it - which is why /tmp is 1777 and\n             * why the check is \"writable by others without a sticky bit\" rather than \"world-writable\".\n             *\n             * Rejecting group-writable outright would be stricter, not safer: /tmp is group-writable on\n             * every conventional system, so a blanket refusal rejects the standard temporary directory\n             * while a 0775 directory with no sticky bit - genuinely unsafe - is refused for a different\n             * reason than the one that matters. One rule, applied to both, is the one that can be\n             * reasoned about.\n             */\n            boolean writableByOthers = (mode & 0022) != 0;\n            if (writableByOthers && !sticky) {\n                throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE,\n                        \"key file directory is writable by others without the sticky bit: \" + component);\n            }\n            if (!isRoot) {\n                requireOwns(component, \"key file directory\");\n            }\n            component = component.getParent();\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Opens a new owner-only ({@code 0600}) output stream, creating the file in the same step that\n     * sets its permissions. The strict decoder is used and the result is re-encoded and compared, so a\n *       non-canonical spelling is refused rather than silently accepted;</li>\n *   <li>no whitespace is accepted <em>anywhere</em>, in either form. A trailing newline is refused in\n *       the text form as well as the raw one: byte {@code 0x0A} is not printable, so it is rejected\n *       before the decoder is consulted. Trimming first is not an option - a value whose leading or\n *       trailing characters are removable is a value whose length somebody else decides, and the length\n *       is what distinguishes the raw form from the text form. The two forms are distinguishable by\n *       length alone, so there is no ambiguity to resolve and no \"try both and hope\" path;</li>\n *   <li>a buffer that is neither is rejected outright. There is no fallback that pads, truncates, or\n *       hashes the input down to 32 bytes - silently deriving a key from a malformed one is how a key\n *       rotation ends up with two different keys called the same thing.</li>\n * </ul>\n *\n * <p>There is deliberately no counterpart that renders key material as a {@code String}: a\n * {@code String} cannot be zeroed, so it would leave the key in the heap past the end of the\n * operation. {@link KeyMaterial#copyForWrite()} is the only export path and it hands back a byte array\n * the caller is expected to wipe.\n */\npublic final class                  \"key material is neither \" + KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES + \" raw bytes nor printable base64url text\");\n            }\n        }\n        String text = new String(in, It is safe to show\n *       to an end user or return over an API, and {@link #getMessage()} returns exactly it, so a\n *       caller who blindly renders {@code e.getMessage()} leaks nothing.</li>\n *   <li>{@link #getTechnicalDetail()} is a one-line, secret-free diagnostic intended for the log.\n *       It is never reachable from {@link #getMessage()} or {@link #toString()}.</li>\n * </ul>\n *\n * <p>Stack traces are disabled: this component has a fixed, small set of failure modes and their\n * causes are already named by {@link Crypto FailureCode}, so a stack trace would add nothing while\n * inviting callers to log internal package layout alongside it.\n *\n * <p>Cryptographic failures in particular never carry the underlying {@code\n * javax.crypto.AEADBadTagException} as a {@code cause} that survives into {@link #getMessage()};\n * the code records it, the detail string describes it, and the key material never does.\n */\npublic final class CryptoException extends Exception {\n\n    private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;\n\n    /** Machine-readable failure category. Safe to expose; it names no secret. */\n    public enum CryptoFailureCode {\n        /** A required input was absent. */\n        MISSING_INPUT(\"No data was supplied\"),\n        /** An input was the wrong type, size, encoding or range. */\n        INVALID_INPUT(\"The supplied data is not in a supported format\"),\n        /** The bytes are not a blob this component produced (unknown or unsupported version). */\n        UNSUPPORTED_FORMAT(\"The supplied data is not in a supported format\"),\n        /** The record's version is known but the caller has no key authorised to read it. */\n        KEY_UNAVAILABLE(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n        /** The key that would be needed is present but not yet usable (not yet valid / destroyed). */\n        KEY_NOT_ACTIVE(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n        /** AES-GCM tag verification failed. Covers a wrong key, a tampered blob and a wrong context. */\n        AUTHENTICATION_FAILED(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n        /** Key material was supplied in a form this component refuses to accept. */\n        INVALID_KEY(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n        /** A key or password source is absent or unusable. Never contains the secret. */\n        KEY_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n        /** A key store or key file failed its ownership or permission requirements. */\n        INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n        /** A ciphertext exceeded {@link Limits#MAX_CIPHERTEXT_BYTES} before anything was allocated. */\n        TOO_LARGE(\"The supplied data is not in a supported format\"),\n        /** The underlying platform refused the primitive. Fails closed; nothing is substituted. */\n        PROVIDER_FAILURE(\"The data could not be processed\");\n\n        private final String userMessage;\n\n        CryptoFailureCode(String userMessage) {\n            this.userMessage = userMessage;\n        }\n\n        /** Generic text for this code. Contains no record, key, path or existence information. */\n        public String userMessage() {\n            return userMessage;\n        }\n    }\n\n    /*\n     * Not transient. `getMessage()` returns `code.userMessage()`, so a code that did not survive\n     * deserialization would make the generic message throw a NullPointerException - on the error path,\n     * where the caller's own handler replaces the safe fixed vocabulary with an unrelated failure. The\n     * enum names only a category, so serializing it discloses nothing. `technicalDetail` stays\n     * transient: it can name a path or a component, and it is meant for the log of the JVM that made it.\n     */\n    private final CryptoFailureCode code;\n    private final transient String technicalDetail;\n\n    CryptoException(String detail) {\n        this(CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT, detail, null);\n    }\n\n    CryptoException(CryptoFailureCode code, String detail) {\n        this(code, detail, null);\n    }\n\n    CryptoException(CryptoFailureCode code, String detail, Throwable cause) {\n        super(code.userMessage(), cause, /*enableSuppression*/ false, /*writableStackTrace*/ false);\n        this.code = code;\n        this.technicalDetail = detail;\n    }\n\n    /** Failure category. */\n    public CryptoFailureCode code() {\n        return code;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Generic, renderable message. Deliberately identical for every failure that concerns a secret,\n     * so a response cannot be used to distinguish a wrong key from a tampered blob.\n     */\n    @Override\n    public String getMessage() {\n        return code.userMessage();\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * One-line diagnostic for logging. Never contains key bytes, passwords, plaintext, file\n     * contents, or a cause's message that might itself contain any of those.\n     */\n    public String getTechnicalDetail() {\n        return technicalDetail;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Writes the generic message only. An instruction to \"use a random\n * password\" with no way to make one safely produces hand-composed values, which is the exact failure\n * the length floor is trying to prevent.\n *\n * <p>Output is a {@code char[]} drawn from the printable ASCII range and contains no whitespace, so it\n * survives a line-oriented config file or an environment variable. It deliberately satisfies\n * {@link KeystoreKeySource#requirePassphrase}: no trailing {@code NUL}, no surrogates.\n *\n * <p><strong>It still needs quoting.</strong> The alphabet is every printable ASCII character except\n * space, which includes {@code $ ` \" ' \\ ! & ; | < > ( ) * ? # ~} - so the value is free of encoding\n * surprises but not of interpretation. Unquoted in a shell it is expanded, split and redirected;\n * unquoted in YAML it can parse as a flow sequence, a mapping or a comment. Handle it as a single-quoted\n * shell argument, or write it to a file created {@code 0600} and pass the path instead. This class does\n * not restrict the alphabet to dodge that, because a narrowed alphabet is what an attacker who could\n * influence generation would choose, and every character dropped costs a bit of entropy; see\n * {@link #ALPHABET_SIZE}.\n *\n * <p>Every character is drawn from a fresh {@link SecureRandom} draw, so there is no dependence on a\n * caller-supplied seed. Strength follows from the true alphabet size: a 48-character output carries\n * 48 * log2({@value #ALPHABET_SIZE}) which is about 315 bits. That is far past brute-force reach and\n * past the point of relevance - the store's defence against an <em>offline</em> attack is the PBKDF2\n * cost in {@link PasswordKeySource}, not this length - so generation is generous rather than minimal.\n *\n * <p>The result is a mutable {@code char[]} rather than a {@code String} precisely so the caller can\n * erase it. Handing back a {@code String} would leave the secret in the interned string heap,\n * un-erasable, for the life of the JVM. Copy it only where necessary and {@code Arrays.fill} the\n * original as soon as the store has been created.\n */\npublic final class RandomPasswords {\n\n    /** Default length: well past the floor any of the key sources impose. */\n    public static final int DEFAULT_LENGTH = 48;\n\n    /** Rejected rather than silently clamped; 4 KiB is the maximum any key source accepts. */\n    public static final int MAX_LENGTH = 1024;\n\n    /**\n     * Printable ASCII, excluding space (which config parsers and shells mishandle). Every remaining\n     * printable character is included: {@code 0x21..0x7E}. Several of them are shell and YAML\n     * metacharacters, which is a quoting obligation on the caller, not a reason to drop them - see the\n     * class note.\n     */\n    private static final char[] ALPHABET =\n            \"!\\\"#$%&'()*+,-./0123456789:;<=>?@ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ[\\\\]^_`abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz{|}~\"\n                    .toCharArray();\n\n    /** Alphabet cardinality, so the strength claim is derived from the alphabet rather than asserted. */\n    public static final int ALPHABET_SIZE = ALPHABET.length;\n\n    private static final SecureRandom RANDOM = new SecureRandom();\n\n    private RandomPasswords() {\n    }\n\n    /** A {@link #DEFAULT_LENGTH}-character random secret. */\n    public static char[] generate() throws CryptoException {\n        return generate(DEFAULT_LENGTH);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * @param length number of characters, {@code 1..}{@value #MAX_LENGTH}; a value outside the range is\n     *     rejected rather than clamped, so a typo cannot quietly produce a 1-character password\n     * @return a fresh {@code char[]} holding the secret. Erase it with {@code Arrays.fill} when done.\n     * @throws CryptoException if {@code length} is out of range or the platform has no random source\n     */\n    public static char[] generate(int length) throws CryptoException {\n        if (length < 1 || length > MAX_LENGTH) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT,\n                    \"length must be between 1 and \" + MAX_LENGTH);\n        }\n        char[] out = new char[length];\n        try {\n            for (int i = 0; i < length; i++) {\n                /*\n                 *        * the alphabet a private detail and cannot leak index patterns.\n                 */\n                out[i] = ALPHABET[RANDOM.nextInt(ALPHABET.length)];\n            }\n            return out;\n        } catch (RuntimeException e) {\n            java.util.Arrays.fill(out, (char) 0);\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.PROVIDER_FAILURE,\n This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.DS-01\n- PR.DS-02\n- PR.DS-10\nmitre_attack:\n- T1600\n- T1573\n- T1553\n- T1486\n---\n# Implementing AES Encryption for Data at Rest\n\n## Overview\n\nAES (Advanced Encryption Standard) is a symmetric block cipher standardized by NIST (FIPS 197) used to protect classified and sensitive data. This skill covers implementing AES-256 encryption in GCM mode for encrypting files and data stores at rest, including proper key derivation, IV/nonce management, and authenticated encryption.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When deploying or configuring implementing aes encryption for data at rest capabilities in your environment\n- When establishing security controls aligned to compliance requirements\n- When building or improving security architecture for this domain\n- When conducting security assessments that require this implementation\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Implement AES-256-GCM encryption and decryption for files\n- Derive encryption keys from passwords using PBKDF2 and Argon2\n- Manage initialization vectors (IVs) and nonces securely\n- Encrypt and decrypt entire directory trees\n- Implement authenticated encryption to detect tampering\n- Handle large files with streaming encryption\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### AES Modes of Operation\n\n| Mode | Authentication | Parallelizable | Use Case |\n|------|---------------|----------------|----------|\n| GCM  | Yes (AEAD)    | Yes            | Network data, file encryption |\n| CBC  | No            | Decrypt only   | Legacy systems, disk encryption |\n| CTR  | No            | Yes            | Streaming encryption |\n| CCM  | Yes (AEAD)    | No             | IoT, constrained environments |\n\n### Key Derivation\n\nNever use raw passwords as encryption keys. Always derive keys using:\n- **PBKDF2**: NIST-approved, widely supported (minimum 600,000 iterations as of 2024)\n- **Argon2id**: Winner of Password Hashing Competition, memory-hard\n- **scrypt**: Memory-hard, good alternative to Argon2\n\n### Nonce/IV Management\n\n- GCM requires a 96-bit (12-byte) nonce that must NEVER be reused with the same key\n- Generate nonces using `os.urandom()` (CSPRNG)\n- Store nonce alongside ciphertext (it is not secret)\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. Install the `cryptography` library: `pip install cryptography`\n2. Generate or derive an encryption key\n3. Create a random nonce for each encryption operation\n4. Encrypt data using AES-256-GCM with the key and nonce\n5. Store nonce + ciphertext + authentication tag together\n6. For decryption, extract nonce, verify tag, and decrypt\n\n## Encrypted File Format\n\n```\n[salt: 16 bytes][nonce: 12 bytes][ciphertext: variable][tag: 16 bytes]\n```\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Always use authenticated encryption (GCM, CCM) to prevent tampering\n- Never reuse a nonce with the same key (catastrophic in GCM)\n- Use at least 256-bit keys for long-term data protection\n- Securely wipe keys from memory after use when possible\n- Rotate encryption keys periodically per organizational policy\n- For disk-level encryption, consider XTS mode (AES-XTS)\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] AES-256-GCM encryption produces valid ciphertext\n- [ ] Decryption recovers original plaintext exactly\n- [ ] Authentication tag detects any ciphertext modification\n- [ ] Key derivation uses sufficient iterations/parameters\n- [ ] Nonces are never reused for the same key\n- [ ] Large files (>1GB) can be processed via streaming\n- [ ] Encrypted file format includes all necessary metadata"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"53b002b9-96b4-42dc-a5d7-373a7ae2f6ed","session_id":"d7e617cc-a2d8-4a3e-b993-59a9fa2fa4b9","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"53b002b9-96b4-42dc-a5d7-373a7ae2f6ed","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now I have both skill checklists. Need line numbers.\n\nF-2: PrivateFiles.requireOwns() uses System.getProperty(\"user.name\") string comparison — spoofable? Actually the process user name is not attacker-controlled typically, but it can mismatch (e.g., numeric owner names, container running as different uid mapping, or when the JVM's user.name property differs from euid, e.g., after `sudo -u` where euid != login name). Actually Java initializes `user.name` from the underlying OS user, typically from the `user.name` property set from LoginContext / `getenv(\"USER\")`... Actually I recall `java_properties` in `java.lang.System.initProperties(java.lang.Boolean)` (Java since JDK 9 rework): In JDK 9+, `System.initProperties()` calls `UserDevices`... I believe `SystemProps.initProperties()` obtains user name via `java.lang.foreign`? No. It does:\n```java\nString userName = System.getenv(\"USER\");  // ?\n```\nLet me not overclaim JDK internals. But regardless of the precise source, comparing `Files.getOwner().getName()` (which resolves the file's UID to a username via the passwd database) with `System.getProperty(\"user.name\")` (a mutable, settable system property; also possibly derived from an env var) is fragile:\n\n- `user.name` can be overridden with `-Duser.name=...` on the command line → trivially spoofs the ownership check.\n- The authoritative comparison should be against the process effective UID, which Java doesn't expose directly; `Files.getOwner()` returns a name, so a robust approach is to compare against the owner of a file known to be owned by the current effective user (e.g., a file just created in the temp dir), or use `id -u` / `/proc/self/status` comparison.\n\nAlso more importantly: the check compares *names*, and the file owner name comes from the UID→name mapping. If the JVM's euid differs from the property (e.g., a setuid launcher, or a container where `$USER` is set to another value), the check produces a false denial (availability) or false accept (if attacker controls env var `USER`... but user.name isn't env-derived in modern JDK — it's from the OS passwd entry for the euid, I believe). To be safe, I'll frame the finding as: the ownership gate is enforced against a settable JVM system property (`-Duser.name=`) rather than the process effective UID, so it can be spoofed/disabled by launch configuration, and it can also mismatch in `sudo -u`/setuid scenarios. CWE-287 Improper Authentication (or CWE-806?). Better: CWE-287 \"Improper Authentication\" — using an attacker-influenceable identifier as the authority for an access decision. Or CWE-1275? Hmm, the classic mapping for \"trusting a self-reported identity string\" is CWE-287 or CWE-290 (Authentication Bypass by Spoofing). I'll use CWE-290: Authentication Bypass by Spoofing. Actually CWE-290 fits: \"an attacker can spoof an identity by controlling a value used for authentication\". And the check is a security control (file-permission gate) whose authority is a settable property.\n\nBut wait — is `-Duser.name` really attacker-controllable? Only if the attacker controls launch args, which usually means they already control the process. Hmm. The more realistic impact: mismatch causes fail-closed (availability), and in shared-hostile scenarios the check is weaker than intended. Let me reconsider severity: Low/Medium. It's a genuine weakness with a defensible fix (compare UIDs via a freshly created probe file or `geteuid()` via `com.sun.security.auth.UnixPrincipal` / `UnixProcessPrincipal`). Actually a clean JDK-only fix: `new com.sun.security.auth.module.UnixSystem().getUid()` — but that's a JDK-internal API. Alternative portable approach: create a temp file in the same directory and compare `Files.getOwner(temp)` with `Files.getOwner(target)` — that reflects the actual euid. That's a good, implementable fix. I'll rate it Medium (the control silently degrades; fail-closed in mismatch, fail-open under `-Duser.name`).\n\nF-3: KeyEncoding.decodeKeyBytes accepts any 32 printable-ASCII bytes as an AES-256 key with no entropy check → a 32-char human passphrase in a key file silently becomes a \"256-bit key\". With the crypto-audit skill's \"Insufficient Entropy\" category, this is a real finding: CWE-326/321? Best: CWE-326 Inadequate Encryption Strength (key strength), or CWE-321 Use of Hard-coded Cryptographic Key (no). Actually \"insufficient entropy in key material\" → CWE-330/CWE-338 relate to RNG; for keys with low entropy, CWE-326 (Inadequate Encryption Strength) is the standard mapping. Also OWASP: \"Use of Cryptographically Weak Key\". I'll use CWE-326, severity Medium. And the fix: require the raw form to come from a validated source, or run the raw key through a KDF/HKDF, or at minimum reject raw text-form-looking input (printable ASCII) so that only truly random 32 bytes are accepted... Careful: rejecting printable ASCII would break legitimate raw keys that happen to be printable (probability ~ (95/256)^32 ≈ 1.6e-13, negligible). A cleaner fix: entropy/Shannon test or require raw keys to fail the printable-ASCII test. Hmm — but that's a heuristic. Alternative defensible fix: apply an extractor (HKDF/SHA-256 with a domain-separation label) over raw key material so a low-entropy value at least... no, that doesn't add entropy. The honest fix: refuse printable-ASCII raw input (force base64url, which forces random generation), and warn/document. Actually there is an even better framing: the ambiguity is between raw 32 bytes and text; since a printable-ASCII 32-byte buffer is indistinguishable from a truncated passphrase, and the class's own doc says entropy comes from the operator, a defensible minimal fix is to reject raw buffers that consist entirely of printable ASCII (an operator using `KeyMaterial.writeTo` gets random bytes which are essentially never all-printable), so any hand-composed value is refused with INVALID_KEY. That's implementable, minimal, and consistent with the file's own design rationale. I'll present that, with the caveat noting the 2^-42 coincidence rate.\n\nLet me compute: probability a uniformly random 32-byte string is all printable ASCII (0x21..0x7E = 94 values of 256) = (94/256)^32 = exp(32*ln(0.367)) = exp(32*(-1.0025)) = exp(-32.08) = 1.2e-14. Fine.\n\nHmm, but wait: would this break existing tests? The review doesn't need to keep tests green (we must not modify the source). But a fix suggestion that breaks tests is fine as long as it's sound; I can note that `Fixtures` may need updating. Actually careful — I should check whether tests generate printable raw keys. Not necessary, but a quick grep would help. Let me check tests for raw 32-byte printable keys.\n\nF-4: KeyVault.sourceFor() holds rotationLock while calling factory.create() → serializes all key loading (PBKDF2 600k–2M iterations, ~0.4–2 s) → DoS amplification. Need line numbers. Severity Medium? It's a real availability concern: an attacker who can trigger decrypts with unknown key versions... hmm, actually the decoy path avoids create for unprovisioned versions. The create path runs once per version (cached thereafter via putIfAbsent). And PasswordKeySource.load() runs PBKDF2 every call? Let me check KeyVault caching: `sourceFor` caches KeyMaterial per version? From summary: `factory.create()` + `putIfAbsent` into `sources`?? Hmm — \"sources\" is the map of KeySource, and there's a `materialCache`? I need to re-read KeyVault to be precise. Let me re-read KeyVault with line numbers around sourceFor.\n\nF-5: Nonce uniqueness is random-only: CWE-323 (nonce reuse). With 96-bit random nonces, birthday bound ~2^32 records per key. Documented as caller responsibility via rotate(). This is a legitimate finding for a crypto library that doesn't enforce a per-key record counter. Severity Medium (the library's own doc admits it). The AES skill's checklist explicitly says \"Nonces are never reused for the same key\" — validation criteria. A fix: track a per-key encryption counter and refuse to encrypt beyond 2^32 records per key version (fail closed), or derive the nonce deterministically from a counter. I'll implement the counter-based fix (deterministic nonce from an AtomicLong + random prefix, or simply refuse past a bound). Good, implementable.\n\nF-6: `SymmetricCrypto(SecureRandom)` accepts a caller-supplied SecureRandom — CWE-338 adjacent. This is arguably by design (injectable for testing). Severity Low. Maybe fold into a finding about \"nonce determinism depends on injected RNG\". Hmm, the constructor validates non-null but cannot verify it's a CSPRNG. A caller-supplied `SecureRandom` seeded deterministically (e.g., `SecureRandom.getInstance(\"SHA1PRNG\")` then `setSeed(fixed)`) yields predictable nonces → catastrophic. Fix: reject RNGs that report... you can't test determinism. Reasonable fix: don't offer the overload, or use `SecureRandom.getInstanceStrong()`... Actually a defensible fix: keep the overload but require the instance to be non-null and document; better fix is to remove the injectable-RNG overload from the public API (make it package-private for tests). I'd report as Low/Medium. I'll include as Low with the fix \"make the overload package-private (test-only) so production callers cannot substitute a seeded RNG\".\n\nF-7: ProviderFailure embeds internal class names in the message; `getMessage()` returns code.userMessage() so it's fine. It's class names only (e.g., \"javax.crypto.AEADBadTagException\"), no secret. Low/informational. Also SymmetricCrypto's PROVIDER_FAILURE detail = cause class name. Not a real leak. I think it's not a vulnerability — class names are not secrets and the message is only in the *cause* returned by getCause(), which is deliberate. com.example.crypto;\n\nimport java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;\nimport java.util.Base64;\n\n/**\n * Parses key material out of a byte buffer that came from configuration or a file.\n *\n * <p>Two shapes are accepted, and only these two:\n * <ol>\n *   <li>exactly {@value KeyMaterial#KEY_BYTES} raw bytes;</li>\n *   <li>canonical base64url text without padding, decoding to exactly\n *       {@value KeyMaterial#KEY_BYTES} bytes, with no whitespace anywhere.</li>\n * </ol>\n *\n * <p>Why the encoding rules are strict:\n * <ul>\n *   <li>{@code Base64.getMimeDecoder()} silently discards every character outside the alphabet, so\n *       {@code \"not-a-key!\"} decodes to five bytes with no error and would become a truncated,\n *       weak key. The strict decoder is used and the result is re-encoded and compared, so a\n *       non-canonical spelling is refused rather than silently accepted;</li>\n *   <li>no whitespace is accepted <em>anywhere</em>, in either form. A trailing newline is refused in\n *       the text form as well as the raw one: byte {@code 0x0A} is not printable, so it is rejected\n *       before the decoder is consulted. Trimming first is not an option - a value whose leading or\n *       trailing characters are removable is a value whose length somebody else decides, and the length\n *       is what distinguishes the raw form from the text form. The two forms are distinguishable by\n *       length alone, so there is no ambiguity to resolve and no \"try both and hope\" path;</li>\n *   <li>a buffer that is neither is rejected outright. There is no fallback that pads, truncates, or\n *       hashes the input down to 32 bytes - silently deriving a key from a malformed one is how a key\n *       rotation ends up with two different keys called the same thing.</li>\n * </ul>\n *\n * <p>There is deliberately no counterpart that renders key material as a {@code String}: a\n * {@code String} cannot be zeroed, so it would leave the key in the heap past the end of the\n * operation. {@link KeyMaterial#copyForWrite()} is the only export path and it hands back a byte array\n * the caller is expected to wipe.\n */\npublic final class KeyEncoding {\n\n    /** Maximum size of a buffer this will look at: more than any accepted key form needs. */\n    public static final int MAX_INPUT_BYTES = 4096;\n\n    private static final not one of the two accepted forms\n     */\n    public static byte[] decodeKeyBytes(byte[] raw) throws CryptoException {\n        byte[] in = Limits.requirePayload(raw, MAX_INPUT_BYTES, \"key material\");\n        if (in.length == KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES) {\n            return in.clone();\n        }\n        if (in.length == 0) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY, \"key material is empty\");\n        }\n        for (byte b : in) {\n            int v = b & 0xFF;\n            /* Control characters, including NUL and newline, are not part of the text form. */\n            if (v <= 0x20 || v > 0x7E) {\n                throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n                        \"key material is neither \" + KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES + \" raw bytes nor printable base64url text\");\n            }\n        }\n        String text = new String(in, StandardCharsets.US_ASCII);\n        byte[] decoded;\n        try {\n            decoded = DECODER.decode(text);\n        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n                    \"key material is not valid base64url text\", e);\n        }\n        if (!CANON.encodeToString(decoded).equals(text)) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n                    \"key material is not canonically encoded base64url text\");\n        }\n        if (decoded.length != KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n                    \"key material decodes to \" + decoded.length + \" bytes, expected \" + KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES);\n        }\n        return decoded;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Renders a key as canonical base64url text. For the one legitimate case: an operator-facing\n     * export used to place a key into a secret manager.\n     *\n     * <p>The result is a {@code String} and therefore <em>not</em> erasable; it exists so the caller\n     * can hand a key to a system that only accepts text. Callers must not log it, must not put it in a\n     * command line, and must not write it to a file they have not created with {@code 0600}\n     * permissions. Prefer {@code PrivateFiles.writeOwnerOnlyFile} with raw bytes.\n     *\n     * <p>{@link KeyMaterial} is required rather than a bare array so the caller has already had to\n     * hold the key in a managed object.\n     */\n    public static String toBase64Url(KeyMaterial key) throws CryptoException {\n        byte[] raw = key.copyForWrite();\n        try {\n            return CANON.encodeToString(raw);\n        } finally {\n            java.util.Arrays.fill(raw, (byte) 0);\n        }\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"e37f1824-d2ff-454e-ba6d-38d97944a14d","session_id":"d7e617cc-a2d8-4a3e-b993-59a9fa2fa4b9","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_cf037732147d48b880c340a5","is_error":false,"content":"Read lines 51-110 of 110 from ../../../../../../../results/qwen/set_a/post_research/2/security/src/main/java/com/example/crypto/KeyEncoding.java"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"a14cc94a-9921-48e4-90cb-fb9cd80107a8","session_id":"d7e617cc-a2d8-4a3e-b993-59a9fa2fa4b9","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"a14cc94a-9921-48e4-90cb-fb9cd80107a8","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now KeyEncoding line numbers: file total 110 lines (dir listing said 109 lines... fine). decodeKeyBytes starts at line 54 (`public static byte[] decodeKeyBytes`). com.example.crypto;\n\nimport java.security.SecureRandom;\n\n/**\n * Generates the random secrets that key stores need - PKCS#12 store and key passwords, or the value of\n * an environment-variable key - so that nobody composes one by hand.\n *\n * <p>Why this is here at all: {@link KeystoreKeySource} requires a password of at least\n * {@link KeystoreKeySource#MIN_PASSPHRASE_CHARS} characters and its real defence is that the password\n * is high-entropy, because PKCS#12's key derivation is cheap. An instruction to \"use a random\n * password\" with no way to make one safely produces hand-composed values, which is the exact failure\n * the length floor is trying to prevent.\n *\n * <p>Output is a {@code char[]} drawn from the printable ASCII range and contains no whitespace, so it\n * survives a line-oriented config file or an environment variable. It deliberately satisfies\n * {@link KeystoreKeySource#requirePassphrase}: no trailing {@code NUL}, no surrogates.\n *\n * <p><strong>It still needs quoting.</strong> The alphabet is every printable ASCII character except\n * space, which includes {@code $ ` \" ' \\ ! & ; | < > ( ) * ? # ~} - so the value is free of encoding\n * surprises but not of interpretation. Unquoted in a shell it is expanded, split and redirected;\n * unquoted in YAML it can parse as a flow sequence, a mapping or a comment. Handle it as a single-quoted\n * shell argument, or write it to a file created {@code 0600} and pass the path instead. This class does\n * not restrict the alphabet to dodge that, because a narrowed alphabet is what an attacker who could\n * influence generation would choose, and every character dropped costs a bit of entropy; see\n * {@link #ALPHABET_SIZE}.\n *\n * <p>Every character is drawn from a fresh {@link SecureRandom} draw, so there is no dependence on a\n * caller-supplied seed. Strength follows from the true alphabet size: a 48-character output carries\n * 48 * log2({@value #ALPHABET_SIZE}) which is about 315 bits. That is far past brute-force reach and\n * past the point of relevance - the store's defence against an <em>offline</em> attack is the PBKDF2\n * cost in {@link PasswordKeySource}, not this length - so generation is generous rather than minimal.\n *\n * <p>The result is a mutable {@code char[]} rather than a {@code String} precisely so the caller can\n * erase it. Handing back a {@code String} would leave the secret in the interned string heap,\n * un-erasable, for the life of the JVM. Copy it only where necessary and {@code Arrays.fill} the\n * original as soon as the store has been created.\n */\npublic final class RandomPasswords {\n\n    /** Default length: well past the floor any of the key sources impose. */\n    public static final int DEFAULT_LENGTH = 48;\n\n    /** Rejected rather than silently clamped; 4 KiB is the maximum any key source accepts. */\n    public static final int MAX_LENGTH = 1024;\n\n    /**\n     * Printable ASCII, excluding space (which config parsers and shells mishandle). Every remaining\n     * printable character is included: {@code 0x21..0x7E}. Several of them are shell and YAML\n     * metacharacters, which is a quoting obligation on the caller, not a reason to drop them - see the\n     * class note.\n     */\n    private static final char[] ALPHABET =\n            \"!\\\"#$%&'()*+,-./0123456789:;<=>?@ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ[\\\\]^_`abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz{|}~\"\n                    .toCharArray();\n\n    /** Alphabet cardinality, so the strength claim is derived from the alphabet rather than asserted. */\n    public static final int ALPHABET_SIZE = ALPHABET.length;\n\n    private static final SecureRandom RANDOM = new SecureRandom();\n\n    private RandomPasswords() {\n    }\n\n    /** A {@link #DEFAULT_LENGTH}-character random secret. */\n    public static char[] generate() throws CryptoException {\n        return generate(DEFAULT_LENGTH);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * @param length number of characters, {@code 1..}{@value #MAX_LENGTH}; a value outside the range is\n     *     rejected rather than clamped, so a typo cannot quietly produce a 1-character password\n     * @return a fresh {@code char[]} holding the secret. Erase it with {@code Arrays.fill} when done.\n     * @throws CryptoException if {@code length} is out of range or the platform has no random source\n     */\n    public static char[] generate(int length) throws CryptoException {\n        if (length < 1 || length > MAX_LENGTH) {\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT,\n                    \"length must be between 1 and \" + MAX_LENGTH);\n        }\n        char[] out = new char[length];\n        try {\n            for (int i = 0; i < length; i++) {\n                /*\n                 * Rejection sampling instead of `random.nextInt(ALPHABET.length)` would be equivalent\n                 * here - nextInt(bound) is already unbiased - but drawing a full int and reducing keeps\n                 * the alphabet a private detail and cannot leak index patterns.\n                 */\n                out[i] = ALPHABET[RANDOM.nextInt(ALPHABET.length)];\n            }\n            return out;\n        } catch (RuntimeException e) {\n            java.util.Arrays.fill(out, (char) 0);\n            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.PROVIDER_FAILURE,\n                    \"a random secret could not be generated: \" + e.getClass().getSimpleName(), e);\n        }\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"5ad552af-7053-44f5-8ea2-91e52dd5e8a8","session_id":"d7e617cc-a2d8-4a3e-b993-59a9fa2fa4b9","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"5ad552af-7053-44f5-8ea2-91e52dd5e8a8","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now pin RandomPasswords line numbers with grep. com.example.crypto;\n\n/**\n * Where a key comes from.\n *\n * <p>Every implementation loads a <em>fresh</em> {@link KeyMaterial} on each call and keeps none\n * cached. That is a deliberate cost: caching key material is the usual way a \"hardened\" component\n * ends up with the key resident in the heap for the life of the JVM, which is exactly what\n * {@link KeyMaterial#destroy()} exists to prevent. Now finalize severity ordering and the finding set. Let me settle the final list:\n\n**High**\n- SEC-01: KeystoreKeySource.load() reads the PKCS#12 store without the post-read identity/ownership re-validation that every other key file goes through → file-swap TOCTOU in a shared directory; the key can be captured/replaced (CWE-367). High. Evidence: KeystoreKeySource.java:201,210,217-223 vs PrivateFiles.java:127-175, PasswordKeySource.java:213.\n\n**Medium**\n- SEC-02: File ownership authorized by `System.getProperty(\"user.name\")` string equality instead of the process UID → spoofable via -Duser.name, so the ownership control (CWE-287 / CWE-806) can be bypassed by an attacker who can set JVM properties... hmm, realistically if attacker can set JVM props they own the process. But it's still wrong-authorization-proxy: e.g., a service running under an account whose *directory* name differs from the file owner name reported by the POSIX layer, or an account renamed; also `user.name` in a forked/derivative process. The real risk: the check claims to prove privacy but compares against an attacker-influenced *system property* (settable by any code in the JVM, e.g. a deserialized library, or via `-Duser.name`), not the real uid. Also, `Files.getOwner().getName()` can return a numeric string like \"1000\" when the id has no name mapping, in which case a legitimate owner is refused (fail-closed). And on a host where the JVM is launched by a wrapper that sets user.name to the invoking sudo user while the effective uid differs, the check passes for the wrong identity. I'll rate Medium. Fix: compare `Files.getOwner` UserPrincipal via `PosixFileAttributes.uid()` against `geteuid()` (e.g. via `sun.misc`? better: use `com.sun.security.auth.module.UnixSystem.getuid()`), falling back to fail-closed.\n\n- SEC-03: KeyVault serializes all cold key loads behind one lock and runs the ~0.4–2.0 s PBKDF2 inside `synchronized (rotationLock)` → attacker-chosen key-version byte in every record turns unauthenticated input into a global serialization point; small forged-record volume stalls every decrypt/encrypt in the process (CWE-400 / CWE-667). Medium. Note: the decoy path avoids running the factory for impossible versions, so the attacker must name a version ≤ highestIssued and below MAX_VERSIONS — which is still enough for versions 1..highestIssued that aren't cached yet. Actually, cold-miss requires a version ≤ highestIssued that isn't provisioned. If factory.create returns a PasswordKeySource for every version ≤ highest, then the KDF runs per new version, and after caching it's warm. So the DoS window is: each distinct version cold-miss costs up to 2s under a global lock; attacker can spray versions 1..highestIssued repeatedly? Once cached, warm. But `sources.size() >= MAX_VERSIONS` then decoy. Also `release()` for non-cached... Hmm — also an attacker who can cause `disable()`/eviction? Let me be accurate: the exposure is (a) the worst case first-touch of each provisioned version blocks all vault ops for up to ~2 s, and (b) repeated concurrent misses on different versions serialize additively; total stall ≈ N × KDF time bounded by MAX_VERSIONS=64 → up to ~2 minutes of serialized stall over the process lifetime, and any rotate() during that window is blocked. That's a genuine but bounded availability concern → Medium/Low. I'll rate Medium and be precise about the bound. Actually I'd rather rate it Low-Medium... The review needs defensible severity. I'll call it Medium with the explicit bound stated.\n\n- SEC-04: KeyEncoding.decodeKeyBytes accepts any 32 raw bytes as an AES-256 key with no strength/entropy check → a 32-char human password passes silently, and it is the only key-entry path that doesn't route through a KDF (CWE-326/CWE-331... more apt: CWE-321? No: CWE-326 is inadequate encryption strength; CWE-331 insufficient entropy. The library's own RandomPasswords/KeystoreKeySource enforce a 32-char floor + high entropy for store passwords, but the file/env key path enforces nothing). I'll use CWE-331 (Insufficient Entropy) as primary with CWE-326 mention. Severity Medium. Fix: reject low-entropy 32-byte raw material unless an explicit opt-out (e.g. require it to be non-canonical-ASCII, or measure Shannon entropy / require ≥ some bits, or refuse raw buffers that are entirely printable ASCII, forcing the base64 form and an explicit \"raw\" API). A clean fix: require an explicit `acceptLowEntropy` flag; default path refuses printable-ASCII-only buffers of exactly 32 bytes.\n\nHmm — careful: raw 32 bytes are legitimately accepted (KeyFileKeySource writes raw bytes with 0600). A 32-char ASCII key is 32 printable bytes → ambiguous with raw form. The strict-but-real fix: route all passphrase-derived keys through PasswordKeySource and reject raw buffers whose bytes are all printable ASCII (since genuine 256-bit random material is 89% likely to contain a non-printable byte... actually probability all 32 random bytes are in 0x21-0x7E = (94/256)^32 ≈ 2^-41, so refusing is safe). That's a neat, defensible fix. Good.\n\n- SEC-05: Key material rendered into un-erasable `String`s inside KeyEncoding (`text` at 70, `CANON.encodeToString(decoded)` at 78, and toBase64Url's return at 104) while the class's own doctrine (javadoc lines 30-34) says no String counterpart is allowed → key persists in heap/cores after use (CWE-316/CWE-226). Also `new String(in, US_ASCII)` on every non-canonical attempt. Severity Low-Medium. I'll call it Low (heap-disclosure prerequisite) — but it's a documented, deliberate trade for toBase64Url. The *undocumented* one is `text` at line 70: parsing a key file's contents into a String — that's the real leak: every env-var/file key load leaves a plaintext copy of the key (as ASCII text) in the heap that cannot be zeroed. For a base64 key in an env var, the env block itself is already un-erasable, so partially moot... but for a base64 key *file*, the load path creates an extra copy. Low.\n\n- SEC-06: Nonce uniqueness is purely probabilistic with no enforcement — no per-key record counter, no ceiling check, rotation left to operator discipline (CWE-323). Severity Medium (documented but unenforced; catastrophic when hit: GCM nonce reuse → confidentiality loss + forgability). Fix: enforce a per-instance/per-key record counter with a hard ceiling.\n\n- SEC-07: `SymmetricCrypto(SecureRandom)` accepts any caller-supplied SecureRandom, including one with a fixed seed or a registered weak SPI, and there's no self-test → nonce generation depends on a config-supplied object with no verification (CWE-338). Low-Medium. I'll make it Low and give a fix that does a seeding self-test/refuses known-weak... Actually a defensible fix: default to a FIPS-ish NativePRNG and, on the injected path, at least verify non-repetition of the first N nonces / require a `SecureRandom` instance not constructed via setSeed-only... Realistic fix: validate by drawing a sample and checking it isn't constant, and document; or better: only accept `SecureRandom` when a caller explicitly opts into \"test mode\". Let me carefully evaluate each candidate defect with fresh eyes, considering README's documented mitigations (which affect severity but not always existence).\n\n**Candidate 1: TOCTOU — `KeystoreKeySource.load()` uses `PrivateFiles.requirePrivateKeyFile(file)` + raw `Files.newInputStream` instead of `PrivateFiles.readOwnerOnlyFile`**\n\nPrivateFiles.readOwnerOnlyFile (line 127+) does:\n1. parent shared check\n2. parentShared → parent inode+mode snapshot\n3. requirePrivateKeyFile(file) — the full check\n4. parentShared → file identity (dev+ino) snapshot\n5. read bytes with size cap\n6. parentShared → re-check: parent identity, file identity (inode swap detection!), file mode, owner.\n\nSo when the parent directory is shared (writable by others), readOwnerOnlyFile defends against the classic race: attacker swaps the file (different inode) between validation and open/read → detected by st_ino mismatch.\n\nKeystoreKeySource.load() line ~201-230: calls `PrivateFiles.requirePrivateKeyFile(file)` (which does pre-open checks: no symlink, regular, mode ≤ 0600, owner, ancestors), then checks size via `Files.size(file)` (need to re-verify), then `try (InputStream in = Files.newInputStream(file))` and `ks.load(in, storePassword)`. There is NO post-read re-validation and NO inode snapshot — because it doesn't go through readOwnerOnlyFile.\n\nAttack: an attacker who can write to the parent directory (shared parent) can, between `requirePrivateKeyFile` and `Files.newInputStream`, replace the key file with their own PKCS#12 keystore (different inode, mode 0600, owned by... wait, ownership check: requirePrivateKeyFile checks the file is owned by \"me\" — the attacker's file would be owned by the attacker, not by me. Hmm. But the race is: validate file F (mine, correct), then attacker does `mv attacker.keystore keyfile`? No — mv of a file they own into a directory they can write: the resulting file would be owned by the attacker (rename preserves owner). Then Files.newInputStream opens the attacker's file. Ownership is NOT re-checked after open. The pre-open check passed on the old inode; the open follows the directory entry to the new inode.\n\nActually wait — can the attacker even rename into the parent dir? If parent is shared (writable by others), yes: rename/replace requires write permission on parent. The file's own mode/owner don't protect against replacement — that's exactly why readOwnerOnlyFile does the post-read inode check (\"an attacker who can move entries in a shared parent can swap the file for another inode\"). The README itself states this exact attack for the generic read path and says it was fixed there. But KeystoreKeySource bypasses readOwnerOnlyFile entirely — it uses requirePrivateKeyFile + Files.newInputStream. So the identical attack applies to the keystore path and is NOT fixed there. Even worse: the keystore contains the key unwrapped with the store password — the attacker could plant a keystore containing a key they know. If the app then \"loads\" that keystore and uses the key... hmm, what would happen? The vault would use the attacker-influenced key for decryption (fails — attacker doesn't know the right key... actually the attacker could *know* the key in their planted keystore! Then the app decrypts records with the attacker's key. Wait, no: decrypt would fail unless the record was encrypted with that key. But encrypt would use the planted key → attacker can decrypt future records! And the version cross-check: KeystoreKeySource's loaded KeyMaterial claims the source's version; planted keystore key would claim the right version. So a file-swap on a shared parent lets an attacker who can write the parent dir substitute the keystore, causing the app to encrypt new records under an attacker-known key. That's a genuine High-ish severity issue (confidentiality break of subsequently encrypted data + potential integrity), conditional on shared-parent + local attacker + race window.\n\nThe mitigation is trivial: the file should be re-validated after load (or the bytes read through readOwnerOnlyFile into a ByteArrayInputStream first, then ks.load from memory). Note KeystoreKeySource already caps size (MAX_STORE_BYTES = 1MB) and reads via InputStream. The clean fix: read the bytes via `PrivateFiles.readOwnerOnlyFile(file, MAX_STORE_BYTES)`, then `ks.load(new ByteArrayInputStream(bytes), storePassword)`. That routes through the inode/owner/mode re-check. That's a clean, minimal, correct fix. \n\nBut wait — is the keystore path really exposed to shared parents? requirePrivateKeyFile already refuses shared ancestor directories? Let me re-check: requirePrivateKeyFile checks ancestors — \"refusing anything writable by others without the sticky bit\". /tmp is 1777 (sticky) → accepted! So a key file under /tmp-like sticky-shared dir passes requirePrivateKeyFile. And in readOwnerOnlyFile, parentShared (sticky 1777 dir → is it \"writable by others\"? yes, sticky bit is what makes it shared-but-safe-ish, and they honor the sticky convention: 1777 accepted) triggers the inode re-check. So exactly: file in a sticky-shared directory (e.g., /tmp) passes validation but is swappable by other users (sticky prevents deletion of others' files... hmm! With sticky bit, other users cannot delete or rename *my* file unless they own it. So in a sticky dir, the attacker can't swap my file!).\n\nHmm, this weakens the TOCTOU argument for sticky dirs. What about non-sticky shared parents? readOwnerOnlyFile refuses parent \"writable by others\" unless sticky... wait, README says: \"refusing anything writable by others without the sticky bit (/tmp is 1777, so the sticky convention is honoured while a genuinely unsafe 0775 is refused)\". So ancestor dirs must be either not world/group-writable, or sticky. In a sticky dir, others cannot unlink/rename my file — BUT they can create another file? No — the race is about replacing the *name* my path resolves to. In a sticky directory, an attacker cannot rename away my file (sticky = only file owner or dir owner can delete/rename entries). So in a sticky dir, name swap by a non-owner attacker is prevented by the sticky bit itself.\n\nThen when does the inode re-check in readOwnerOnlyFile actually matter? Cases: (a) dir is group-writable without sticky but group... no, refused. (b) dir mode like 0770 shared with group? Refused (\"writable by others\" — what counts? Let me check isSharedWritable: probably checks group-write and other-write of dirs, with sticky allowance). Hmm — the code's exact ancestor rule needs re-reading: \"every ancestor directory checked — refusing anything writable by others without the sticky bit\". If a dir is group-writable (0775) and there is no sticky bit → refused. If 1777 → allowed. If 0775 + sticky (1775) → allowed? The sticky convention honored: writable-by-group-but-sticky? Let me re-read PrivateLines fully before finalizing.\n\nAlso for the sticky case: even if attacker can't swap my file, they can pre-create... no, CREATE_NEW is only on the write path.\n\nSo how strong is finding #1? The inode re-check exists in the codebase precisely because the authors considered swap-in-shared-parent a real threat on their threat model. Whether the ancestor checks fully close it, the codebase's own doctrine is: after reading through a shared parent, re-verify identity. KeystoreKeySource does not do that re-verification, so if ANY shared configuration passes the ancestor check (sticky dirs, or group-shared dirs where the check passes), the keystore path is exposed while the generic path is not. And result.md explicitly lists `inode-recheck-dropped` as a surviving mutation — but that mutation was against readOwnerOnlyFile. The keystore path never had the recheck to drop. This is a real asymmetry: same file-validation helper family, one path hardened, one not. I'll report as a finding (High or Medium-High), framed precisely: post-read re-verification (inode/mode/owner) performed by readOwnerOnlyFile when the parent is shared is entirely absent from KeystoreKeySource.load(), which opens the raw stream after a pre-only check; a same-host attacker able to alter the directory entry in the window (e.g. any config where a shared-but-sticky or group dir passes) can feed a substituted keystore that is then used as if it were the validated file. Need to verify.\n\n**Candidate 2: `user.name` system property as identity** — still valid: requireOwns compares `Files.getOwner(...)` name to `System.getProperty(\"user.name\")`. On Linux, \"user.name\" is initialized from the OS at JVM startup but can be overridden on the command line (`-Duser.name=...`), and if overridden, the ownership gate compares against a wrong name → either false pass (if attacker can make files owned by the spoofed name... they'd need that uid) or false refusal. The real risk is inverted: if user.name is spoofable by the *caller's own launch config*, an attacker with the ability to modify launch flags already controls the process. More realistic defect: correctness — on some systems Files.getOwner().getName() returns a numeric uid string when the name isn't resolvable, while user.name gives the login name → mismatch → fail closed (availability, not security). And there's no check against ProcessHandle... Java has no direct getuid API (JEP \"Foreign function & Memory\" needed). So using user.name is the common JDK-idiomatic approach; the residual is spoofability via -Duser.name. Severity Low-Medium. The check is an authorization/ownership decision made on a user-controlled identity claim. CWE-287 \"Improper Authentication\" fits loosely. THAT is the concrete attack: any code executing in the same JVM can `System.setProperty(\"user.name\", \"victim\")` and defeat the ownership gate. But any code in the same JVM can already read the key... so this is privilege-model circular. Still, defense-in-depth: cache at static init. Also on Windows getOwner works differently.
